          Information about Copyrighted Content
Some instruments administered for studies archived with ICPSR may contain in whole
or substantially in part contents from copyrighted instruments. Reproductions of the
instruments are provided as documentation for the analysis of the data associated with
this collection. Restrictions on "fair use" apply to all copyrighted content. More information
about the reproduction of copyrighted works by educators and librarians is available from
the United States Copyright Office.




                                NOTICE
               WARNING CONCERNING COPYRIGHT RESTRICTIONS

The copyright law of the United States (Title 17, United States Code) governs the making
of photocopies or other reproductions of copyrighted material. Under certain conditions
specified in the law, libraries and archives are authorized to furnish a photocopy or other
reproduction. One of these specified conditions is that the photocopy or reproduction is
not to be "used for any purpose other than private study, scholarship, or research." If a
user makes a request for, or later uses, a photocopy or reproduction for purposes in
excess of "fair use," that user may be liable for copyright infringement.

Thank you for participating in this survey. All of your answers are completely confidential and anonymous. Please
answer every question as truthfully as possible. This is not a race! Take your time to read each question and
provide your honest opinion. Thank you very much.

3 0 1 which will ensure the data is input in a uniform manner

Programmer: If respondent does not enter “X” (upper or lower is fine) as first character, return this prompt:

It looks like you did not enter your zip code correctly, please read the instructions carefully below.

For the software to read this form, please mark an “X” as the first digit before your zip code, such as: X 9 1 3 0 1
which will ensure the data is input in a uniform manner

[ ] [ ] [ ] [ ] [ ] [ ]


Note: The above item, S15 serves two purposes, first it provides us with respondent zip code; and second, it serves
as a soft attention check. For respondents who are not reading carefully, it gives them a reminder to read and pay
attention – similar to filing out online forms – however it does not eliminate any respondents and it does not send
the message that we are reading and monitoring their opinions

Okay – great! Let’s move on to the main survey



QUESTION LEGEND

No letter indicator = common question asked of all racial groups

S=Screening questions
SPLIT A / SPLIT B = split sample asked of random ½ of sample
L=Any respondent who self-identifies as Latino
B= Any respondent who self-identifies as Black
A= Any respondent who self-identifies as Asian Americans/Native Hawaiians/Pacific Islanders
W= Any respondent who self-identifies as White, non-Hispanics
BI= Any respondent who self-identifies as Black immigrant
AF= Any respondent who self-identifies as Afro Latino
Q= Any respondent who self-identifies as LGBTQ
N= Any respondent who self-identifies as Native American/American Indian
M= Any respondent who self-identifies as MENA*
H= Any respondent who self-identifies as Native Hawaiian
Y=Any respondent age 16 or 17 only

Respondents who indicate multiple identities will receive multiple types of group questions
* Religion item is asked early in survey, below at Q58, before any Muslim-specific content




Principal Investigators: Lorrie Frasure, Janelle Wong, Edward D. Vargas, Matt Barreto {final 4.28.2021}         p.9

for both voting methods. Also, randomize order of voting methods; each respondent completes both voting
methods.]

voting.

[Split B.1: RCV-Long-4] One way of voting uses a ranked vote, in which you rank candidates to indicate your first
choice, your second choice, and so on. If a candidate receives a majority of first choice votes then that person
wins. If no candidate has a majority of votes then the last place candidate is eliminated and that candidate’s first
choice votes are transferred to the next choice. So, if your preferred candidate is eliminated then your vote is
transferred to your next choice. This process of eliminating candidates and transferring votes continues until a
candidate has a majority of votes and that person wins the election.

For example, if Joe Biden decides not to run for re-election and the 2024 presidential election was today which
candidate would you choose? Indicate your first choice in the first column, your second choice in the second
column, and so no. [Randomize order of candidates]

                                                                       1st Choice    2nd Choice       3rd Choice
 Candidate
 Donald Trump, Jr., Republican
 Nikki Haley, former Republican governor of South Carolina and
 ambassador to the UN
 Andrew Cuomo, Democratic governor of New York

 Kamala Harris, former Democratic California Senator and Vice-
 President elect




Principal Investigators: Lorrie Frasure, Janelle Wong, Edward D. Vargas, Matt Barreto {final 4.28.2021}            p.36
                    CMPS: Collaborative Multiracial Post-election Survey 2020

choice, your second choice, and so on. If a candidate receives a majority of first choice votes then that person
wins. If no candidate has a majority of votes then the last place candidate is eliminated and that candidate’s first
choice votes are transferred to the next choice. So, if your preferred candidate is eliminated then your vote is
transferred to your next choice. This process of eliminating candidates and transferring votes continues until a
candidate has a majority of votes and that person wins the election.

candidate would you choose? Indicate your first choice in the first column, your second choice in the second

377.   [W] We are interested in your reactions to some things that happen in everyday life. We will first describe
   the situation to you and then ask you a few questions.

Mrs. Lewis, a White woman with young children, posts advertisements for a nanny on community bulletin boards.
She receives many inquiries and decides to interview all applicants over the phone. Mrs. Lewis is most impressed
with a woman named Laurette, who has relevant experience, is an excellent cook, and comes enthusiastically
recommended. Mrs. Lewis invites Laurette over for what she expects will be the final step of the hiring process.
When Laurette arrives, Mrs. Lewis is surprised to see that Laurette is Black. After Laurette's visit, which goes very
well, Mrs. Lewis thanks her for her time but says that she will not be offered the job. When Laurette asks why,
Mrs. Lewis says that she doesn't think that her children would feel comfortable around her. Laurette is upset
about Mrs. Lewis' actions. How much sympathy do you feel for Laurette?

                                                                     A great deal of sympathy .................................................. 1
                                                                     A lot of sympathy .............................................................. 2
                                                                     Some sympathy................................................................. 3
                                                                     A little sympathy ............................................................... 4
                                                                     I do not feel any sympathy for Laurette ........................... 5

379.   [W] Milford is a mid-sized city in the Northeast. The main bus depot for the city is located in the Whittier

   evidence of the bus depot's harmful effects. The Milford Department of Transportation officials, who are
   mostly White, state that Whittier is the best location for the depot because it is centrally located and many
   Whittier residents take the bus. Furthermore, it would be expensive to relocate the bus depot to a new
   location. Whittier community leaders are very upset by the Department's inaction. How much sympathy do
   you feel for the residents of Whittier?

Now we have some questions about different groups in our society.
We're going to show you a seven‐point scale on which the characteristics of the people in a group can be rated.
In the first statement a score of '1' means that you think almost all of the people in that group tend to be
'peaceful.' A score of '7' means that you think most people in the group are 'violent.'
A score of '4' means that you think that most people in the group are not closer to one end or the other, and of
course, you may choose any number in between. [Randomize]

[Slide Widget, 1= Peaceful to 7=Violent]

protesting against unfair treatment of Black people. A score of '7' means that you think these protests were only
about vandalism and looting. A score of '4' means that you think that the Black Lives Matter protests were about
both of these aspects, equally and of course, you may choose any number in between.

799.    [SPLIT A] When we think of people in racial or ethnic terms, we usually use the labels White, Black Latino,
    Asian,American Indian, Native Hawaiian, Pacific Islander or Middle Eastern. Sometimes one label is sufficient
    and sometimes we use more than one racial or ethnic label. Imagine if we used a 10 point system to define
    our race and ethnicity, where the 10 points are assigned to whichever racial or ethnic groups we think
    accurately describes a person.

For example, if you think of someone as half-White and half-Black, you might allocate 5 points to White and 5
points to Black. Or if you think of someone as mostly White but with some Black heritage, you might allocate 7 or
8 points to White and 2 or 3 points to Black.




Principal Investigators: Lorrie Frasure, Janelle Wong, Edward D. Vargas, Matt Barreto {final 4.28.2021}                                       p.118
                    CMPS: Collaborative Multiracial Post-election Survey 2020

800.    [SPLIT B] When we think of people in racial or ethnic terms, we usually use the labels White, Black Latino,
    Asian, American Indian, Native Hawaiian, Pacific Islander or Middle Eastern... Sometimes one label is
    sufficient and sometimes we use more than one racial or ethnic label. Imagine if we used a 10 point system to
    define our race and ethnicity, where the 10 points are assigned to whichever racial or ethnic groups we think
    accurately describes a person.

For example, if you think of someone as just Black, you would allocate all 10 points to Black. Or if you think of
someone as mixed race, but more White than Black, you might allocate 7 or 8 points to White and 2 or 3 points to
Black.

                           2020 CMPS Survey Instrument —2020 CMPS Errata

*2020 CMPS Survey Instrument is to be used in conjunction with the 2020 CMPS Errata.

The 2020 CMPS Errata that clarifies any inconsistencies (i.e. letters or numbering) between the 2020 CMPS
Final Survey Instrument/Codebook and the Programmed Survey).

The 2020 CMPS Survey Instrument and the programmed instrument have been updated to correct minor
errors and errata concerning differences in how each was originally recorded. Specifically, the 2020 CMPS
Errata documents align the codebook and survey instrument with respect to the numerical ordering, variable
labels, and exact response option text for the items contained in the CMPS codebook and the CMPS instrument.

These edits were necessary to account for minor changes made over the course of the project to accommodate
various needs related to fielding the survey. Between the drafting and the programming of the 2020 CMPS there
were modifications made to the instrument to provide more complete responses to our users. We therefore made
changes to the stated text and formatting of some questions in these documents. A common example of why this
was necessary was to allow multiple responses which resulted in more than one programmed item.

In other instances, questions were grouped together and randomized to ensure that question ordering did not
influence the respondents. When these questions were randomized, the regrouping may have altered the
question numbering. Several items allowed for respondents to mark ‘other’, and then fill in an open-ended
prompt. These open-ended responses are in the released data as their own response item.

This document notes the original question numbering and is followed by the new question numbering. These
are both in bold for ease of reference.




                                                                                                              1
*2020 CMPS Survey Instrument is to be used in conjunction with the 2020 CMPS Errata.

[Split B.1: RCV-Long-4] One way of voting uses a ranked vote, in which you rank candidates to indicate your first choice,
your second choice, and so on. If a candidate receives a majority of first choice votes then that person wins. If no
candidate has a majority of votes then the last place candidate is eliminated and that candidate’s first choice votes are
transferred to the next choice. So, if your preferred candidate is eliminated then your vote is transferred to your next
choice. This process of eliminating candidates and transferring votes continues until a candidate has a majority of votes
and that person wins the election.

For example, if Joe Biden decides not to run for re-election and the 2024 presidential election was today which candidate
would you choose? Indicate your first choice in the first column, your second choice in the second column, and so no.
[Randomize order of candidates]

Donald Trump, Jr., Republican……………………………………………………………………………………….. Q195B1r1
Nikki Haley, former Republican governor of South Carolina and ambassador to the UN……….. Q195B1r2
Andrew Cuomo, Democratic governor of New York………………………………………………………….. Q195B1r3
Kamala Harris, former Democratic California Senator and Vice-President elect……………………. Q195B1r4

[Split B.2: RCV-Long-10] One way of voting uses a ranked vote, in which you rank candidates to indicate your first choice,
your second choice, and so on. If a candidate receives a majority of first choice votes then that person wins. If no
candidate has a majority of votes then the last place candidate is eliminated and that candidate’s first choice votes are
transferred to the next choice. So, if your preferred candidate is eliminated then your vote is transferred to your next
choice. This process of eliminating candidates and transferring votes continues until a candidate has a majority of votes
and that person wins the election.
